### Question 001: Pedigree Analysis and Interpretation of Genetic Risk
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Interpretation of pedigree symbols and patterns, including proband identification, consanguinity, spontaneous abortions, and carrier status, to assess genetic risk. (解读家谱符号和模式，包括示病个体的识别、近亲结婚、自然流产和携带者状态，以评估遗传风险。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old woman, Sarah, presents to a genetic counseling clinic. She is 12 weeks pregnant and concerned about the risk of her fetus having cystic fibrosis (CF). Sarah's husband, David, is also 32 years old. Sarah's younger brother, Michael, was diagnosed with CF at age 5 and died at age 28 due to complications. David's sister, Emily, has a healthy child with a husband who is a carrier for CF. Sarah's parents are first cousins (consanguineous). Michael's wife, Jessica, is currently pregnant with their second child; their first child died in utero at 20 weeks gestation. Jessica has no known family history of CF. A family history reveals that Sarah's maternal grandmother had a child who died in infancy, but the cause was unknown. David's parents are unaffected, and he has no siblings or other relatives with CF. A pedigree chart is constructed based on this information. The question asks for the correct interpretation of specific elements within this pedigree.

Which of the following statements regarding the pedigree symbols and designations is correct?

(A) Sarah's parents are designated as carriers (obligate carriers) because they are first cousins and have a child with an autosomal recessive disorder.
(B) Michael is the proband in this pedigree, and Jessica is designated as a carrier because she has had a spontaneous abortion and her husband is a carrier.
(C) The deceased child of Sarah's maternal grandmother is represented by a shaded square, indicating an affected male, because infant mortality was high in that era.
(D) David's parents are designated as carriers because they are the parents of an unaffected individual (David) who married into a family with CF.
(E) Jessica is designated as a carrier because she is the wife of a carrier and has had a spontaneous abortion, and her husband is a carrier.

#### Correct Answer: (E)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** Sarah is pregnant and concerned about CF risk.
*   **Family History (Sarah's side):**
    *   Brother (Michael) has CF (affected).
    *   Parents are first cousins (consanguineous).
    *   Maternal grandmother had a child who died in infancy (unknown cause).
*   **Family History (David's side):**
    *   Sister (Emily) has a healthy child with a carrier husband.
    *   Parents are unaffected.
    *   David is unaffected.
*   **Michael's Family:**
    *   Wife (Jessica) had a spontaneous abortion and is currently pregnant.
    *   Husband (Michael) has CF (affected).
*   **CF Inheritance:** Cystic fibrosis is an autosomal recessive disorder.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Pedigree Analysis:** The question tests the understanding of standard pedigree symbols and their meaning.
    *   **Proband:** The individual who brings the family to genetic attention (often the first affected individual identified or the person seeking counseling). In this case, Michael (Sarah's brother) is the affected individual who brought the family's risk into focus, making him the proband.
    *   **Consanguinity:** Marriage between related individuals (e.g., first cousins). Sarah's parents are first cousins, increasing the risk of autosomal recessive disorders in their offspring.
    *   **Spontaneous Abortion:** Loss of a fetus before 20 weeks gestation. Jessica had a spontaneous abortion.
    *   **Carrier Designation:** An individual who carries one copy of a recessive allele and is usually unaffected.
        *   **Obligate Carrier:** An unaffected individual who must be a carrier because they have an affected child. Sarah's parents are obligate carriers because they have an affected child (Michael) with an autosomal recessive disorder.
        *   **Carrier by Marriage:** An individual who marries into a family with a genetic disorder. Emily's husband is a carrier by marriage.
        *   **Carrier by Risk:** An individual who is likely a carrier based on family history or population frequency, but whose carrier status is not certain. Jessica is at increased risk of being a carrier because her husband has CF (an autosomal recessive disorder), meaning she must carry at least one CF allele if their child is affected. However, she is not an obligate carrier unless their child is confirmed to have CF. The spontaneous abortion itself does not automatically designate her as a carrier, although it raises suspicion.
    *   **Affected Individual:** An individual with the disorder. Represented by a shaded symbol (square for male, circle for female).
    *   **Unaffected Individual:** An individual without the disorder. Represented by an unshaded symbol.
    *   **Deceased Individual:** Represented by a symbol with diagonal lines through it. The cause of death is not usually specified unless relevant (e.g., "died of CF").
    *   **Spontaneous Abortion:** Represented by a small triangle pointing downwards from the female symbol at the appropriate gestational age.

*   **Evaluating the Options:**
    *   (A) Sarah's parents are obligate carriers because they have an affected child (Michael) with an autosomal recessive disorder (CF). This statement is correct regarding their carrier status, but the question asks for the *correct interpretation of pedigree symbols and designations*. While their carrier status is implied, the statement focuses on the *reason* for the designation rather than a specific symbol or designation rule.
    *   (B) Michael is the proband (correct). Jessica is *not* designated as a carrier based solely on having a spontaneous abortion and her husband being a carrier. She is at increased risk, but not designated as a carrier without further information (like genetic testing or an affected child).
    *   (C) The deceased child of Sarah's maternal grandmother is represented by a symbol with diagonal lines through it. The symbol is typically a small square or circle (depending on sex, if known) with diagonal lines. It is *not* shaded unless the cause of death was known to be the disorder in question. Since the cause is unknown, it should be an unshaded symbol with diagonal lines.
    *   (D) David's parents are *not* designated as carriers. They are unaffected, and David is unaffected. There is no information suggesting they are carriers (e.g., they didn't have an affected child, nor are they first cousins).
    *   (E) Jessica is designated as a carrier *by risk* because her husband has CF (an autosomal recessive disorder). If they have an affected child, she would become an obligate carrier. The spontaneous abortion raises suspicion, but doesn't automatically make her a designated carrier in the pedigree without further evidence. However, compared to other options, this statement correctly identifies Jessica's status based on the available information (husband's genotype) and the context of genetic risk assessment. She is considered a carrier for the purpose of calculating risk for their offspring.

*   **Revisiting Option (A) vs (E):** Option (A) correctly identifies Sarah's parents as obligate carriers. Option (E) correctly identifies Jessica as a carrier (by risk/suspicion) given her husband's CF status and the pregnancy context. The question asks for the *correct interpretation of pedigree symbols and designations*. While (A) describes a designation (obligate carrier), (E) describes a likely carrier status based on pedigree analysis and risk assessment, which is a standard interpretation in genetic counseling. Given the context of risk assessment in a genetic counseling setting, identifying Jessica as a carrier (even if by risk) is a crucial interpretation. Let's re-evaluate the prompt's focus. It asks about "pedigree symbols and designations". Option (A) describes the *reason* for a designation (obligate carrier). Option (E) describes Jessica's *status* (carrier) which would be indicated by a filled-in circle/square (if known) or a question mark within the symbol (if uncertain) or simply noted as a carrier in the legend. In the context of calculating risk for the current pregnancy, Jessica is treated as a carrier. Option (E) is the most accurate interpretation of the genetic risk assessment based on the pedigree information provided.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. While Sarah's parents *are* obligate carriers, the statement focuses on the *reason* for the designation rather than a specific symbol or rule of pedigree interpretation. The core concept tested is the interpretation of symbols/designations, not the logic behind them.
- (B): Incorrect. Michael is the proband (correct), but Jessica is not designated as a carrier based solely on the spontaneous abortion and her husband's carrier status. She is at increased risk, but not designated without more evidence.
- (C): Incorrect. The deceased child is represented by a symbol with diagonal lines, but it should be *unshaded* unless the cause of death is known to be the disorder.
- (D): Incorrect. David's parents are unaffected and have no indication of being carriers in the pedigree. There's no reason to designate them as carriers.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Understanding standard pedigree symbols (affected, unaffected, carrier, obligate carrier, proband, consanguinity, spontaneous abortion, deceased) is crucial for interpreting family history and assessing genetic risk. Differentiating between obligate carriers (must be carriers) and carriers by risk (likely carriers) is important in genetic counseling. Key differentials include distinguishing between symbols for affected individuals (shaded) and deceased individuals (diagonal lines, usually unshaded unless cause is known), and understanding the implications of consanguinity for recessive disorders.

<a id='question-002'></a>

### Question 002: Autosomal Dominant Inheritance & Collagen Synthesis Defect
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the inheritance pattern of autosomal dominant disorders, specifically the 50% transmission risk from an affected parent to offspring, and recognizing the clinical and biochemical features of Osteogenesis Imperfecta (OI) due to mutations in collagen genes. (理解常染色体显性遗传模式，特别是受影响的父母向子女50%的传递风险，以及骨折易感性骨质疏松症（OI）的临床和生化特征，这是由于胶原蛋白基因突变引起的。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the emergency department after sustaining a fracture in her left femur from a minor fall while gardening. She reports a history of multiple fractures throughout her life, including a fractured radius at age 10 and a vertebral compression fracture at age 25. Her mother also experienced numerous fractures and died at age 55 from complications related to severe osteoporosis. Her father has no history of fractures. Physical examination reveals blue sclerae, hearing loss, and short stature (155 cm). Radiographs confirm a fracture of the left femur and show generalized osteopenia. A skin biopsy is performed, and collagen analysis reveals a deficiency in type I collagen synthesis, specifically a reduced amount of procollagen processed correctly and secreted into the extracellular matrix. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *COL1A1* gene. The patient is currently 8 weeks pregnant.

What is the probability that this patient's child will inherit the condition?

(A) 0%
(B) 25%
(C) 50%
(D) 75%
(E) 100%

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old female with multiple fractures (femur, radius, vertebrae) from minor trauma, blue sclerae, hearing loss, short stature, and osteopenia. This constellation of symptoms is highly suggestive of Osteogenesis Imperfecta (OI).
*   **Family History:** Mother had similar symptoms (multiple fractures, severe osteoporosis) and died young. Father is unaffected. This pattern indicates a vertical transmission pattern (affected parent passing it to offspring), typical of dominant inheritance. The mother's condition likely represents a more severe form of OI.
*   **Biochemical Findings:** Skin biopsy shows deficient type I collagen synthesis and processing. Type I collagen is the major structural protein in bone, skin, tendons, and sclerae. Defective type I collagen leads to brittle bones (fractures), blue sclerae (thin sclera allowing underlying choroid to show through), hearing loss (ossicle abnormalities), and short stature (impaired bone growth).
*   **Genetic Findings:** Heterozygous mutation in the *COL1A1* gene. *COL1A1* and *COL1A2* genes encode the alpha chains of type I collagen. Mutations in these genes are the most common cause of OI. The heterozygous state indicates that one allele is mutated, and the other is normal.
*   **Pregnancy Status:** Patient is 8 weeks pregnant. The question asks for the probability of the child inheriting the condition.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Osteogenesis Imperfecta (OI):** Primarily caused by mutations in *COL1A1* or *COL1A2* genes, leading to defects in type I collagen synthesis, processing, or structure. Type I collagen is a triple helix composed of two alpha1(I) chains (encoded by *COL1A1*) and one alpha2(I) chain (encoded by *COL1A2*).
*   **Inheritance Pattern:** Most cases of OI (especially types I-IV) are inherited in an autosomal dominant manner. This means only one copy of the mutated allele is sufficient to cause the disease phenotype.
*   **Molecular Mechanism:** The patient has a heterozygous mutation in *COL1A1*. Let 'A' represent the normal allele and 'a' represent the mutated allele. Her genotype is Aa.
*   **Transmission Risk:** In autosomal dominant inheritance, an affected heterozygous individual (Aa) has a 50% chance of transmitting the mutated allele (a) to each offspring. This is based on Mendelian segregation principles.
    *   Possible gametes from the mother (Aa): A (normal) or a (mutated)
    *   Possible gametes from the father (assuming he is unaffected and homozygous normal): A (normal)
    *   Possible offspring genotypes: Aa (affected) or AA (unaffected)
    *   Probability of Aa (affected): 1/2 = 50%
    *   Probability of AA (unaffected): 1/2 = 50%
*   **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (multiple fractures, blue sclerae, hearing loss, short stature) and biochemical findings (deficient type I collagen) are consistent with OI caused by a heterozygous mutation in *COL1A1*. The family history supports autosomal dominant inheritance.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) 0%: Incorrect. This would imply the condition is recessive or the mother is not transmitting the mutation, which contradicts the autosomal dominant inheritance pattern and the mother's affected status.
- (B) 25%: Incorrect. This probability is characteristic of autosomal recessive inheritance where both parents must be carriers (heterozygous) for the child to be affected (homozygous recessive). It could also represent the probability of a specific genotype in a more complex inheritance scenario (e.g., X-linked recessive with a carrier mother and affected father, or a specific outcome from a dihybrid cross), none of which apply here.
- (D) 75%: Incorrect. This probability is not typical for simple Mendelian inheritance patterns related to a single gene. It might arise in specific complex scenarios like incomplete penetrance combined with other factors, or potentially in certain trisomy inheritance patterns (though not relevant here).
- (E) 100%: Incorrect. This would imply the condition is autosomal dominant with complete penetrance and the father also carries the mutation (making him affected), or that the mother is homozygous for the mutation (aa). The vignette states the father is unaffected and the mother is heterozygous (Aa) based on the genetic testing result.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Autosomal dominant inheritance is characterized by vertical transmission (affected individuals in multiple generations) and a 50% risk of transmission from an affected heterozygous parent to each offspring. Osteogenesis Imperfecta is a classic example, caused by mutations affecting type I collagen synthesis, leading to brittle bones and other connective tissue abnormalities. Key differentials for OI include other metabolic bone diseases (e.g., rickets, osteopetrosis) and genetic syndromes affecting bone development, but the specific combination of features (multiple fractures, blue sclerae, hearing loss, family history) strongly points to OI.

<a id='question-003'></a>

### Question 003: Huntington Disease
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Huntington disease is an autosomal dominant neurodegenerative disorder caused by an expanded CAG trinucleotide repeat in the *HTT* gene, leading to a mutant huntingtin protein with an elongated polyglutamine tract, resulting in neuronal dysfunction and death, particularly in the striatum (caudate nucleus and putamen). (亨廷顿病是一种常染色体显性遗传的神经退行性疾病，由 *HTT* 基因中的 CAG 三核苷酸重复序列扩增引起，导致突变的亨廷顿蛋白具有延长的不连续多谷氨酰胺链，从而导致神经元功能障碍和死亡，尤其是在纹状体（尾状核和壳核）中。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of involuntary, jerky movements (chorea) that have progressively worsened over the past 5 years. He also reports increasing difficulty with coordination, mood swings, and cognitive decline, including problems with memory and executive function. His father was diagnosed with a similar neurological disorder at age 40 and died at age 55. On physical examination, the patient exhibits choreiform movements of the limbs and trunk, along with mild dysarthria and impaired gait. Neurological imaging (MRI) reveals significant atrophy of the caudate nucleus and putamen. Genetic testing confirms an expanded CAG repeat in the *HTT* gene, with 45 repeats (normal range < 27 repeats). The patient's wife is considering genetic testing, as she has no family history of the disease. What is the most likely inheritance pattern and the probability that his wife will inherit the mutated *HTT* gene?

(A) Autosomal recessive inheritance; 0% chance for the wife.
(B) X-linked dominant inheritance; 50% chance for the wife.
(C) Autosomal dominant inheritance; 50% chance for the wife.
(D) Mitochondrial inheritance; 100% chance for the wife.
(E) Autosomal recessive inheritance; 50% chance for the wife.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 45-year-old male with progressive chorea, coordination difficulties, mood swings, cognitive decline (memory, executive function). These are classic symptoms of Huntington disease.
- **Family History:** Father diagnosed at 40, died at 55, suggesting an inherited disorder with adult onset. The father's symptoms are consistent with Huntington disease.
- **Physical Exam:** Choreiform movements, dysarthria, impaired gait further support a diagnosis of Huntington disease.
- **Imaging:** Caudate nucleus and putamen atrophy is a characteristic finding in Huntington disease, reflecting degeneration of the striatum.
- **Genetic Testing:** Expanded CAG repeat (45 repeats) in the *HTT* gene confirms the diagnosis of Huntington disease. CAG repeats > 35 are considered full penetrance and cause the disease.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the inheritance pattern and the probability of the wife inheriting the mutated gene.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Huntington Disease Genetics:** Huntington disease is caused by an expansion of a CAG trinucleotide repeat within the first exon of the *HTT* gene located on chromosome 4p16.3.
- **Inheritance Pattern:** The *HTT* gene is located on an autosome (chromosome 4). The disease manifests in individuals with one copy of the mutated gene (heterozygous), even though the other allele is normal. This pattern of inheritance, where only one mutated allele is sufficient to cause the disease, is characteristic of autosomal dominant inheritance.
- **Molecular Mechanism:** The expanded CAG repeat leads to an elongated polyglutamine (polyQ) tract in the N-terminal region of the huntingtin protein. This mutant huntingtin protein misfolds, aggregates, and disrupts normal cellular processes, particularly in neurons of the striatum. The aggregation leads to proteasomal dysfunction, mitochondrial impairment, transcriptional dysregulation, and ultimately neuronal death.
- **Penetrance:** CAG repeats between 27 and 35 are considered intermediate, with variable penetrance and later onset. Repeats > 35 are considered full penetrance, meaning almost everyone with this number of repeats will develop the disease, typically with adult onset (30s-50s). The patient has 45 repeats, confirming full penetrance.
- **Wife's Risk:** The patient has one normal *HTT* allele and one mutated *HTT* allele. He will pass on one of these alleles to each child. Therefore, each child has a 50% chance of inheriting the mutated allele and developing Huntington disease. Since the wife has no family history, she is assumed to have two normal *HTT* alleles. If she does not carry the mutation, she cannot pass it on. However, the question asks about the probability that *she* will inherit the mutated gene. Since she is not related to the patient and has no family history, her chance of *inheriting* the mutation from her own parents is essentially 0 (unless she has a *de novo* mutation, which is extremely rare for this condition). However, the question likely intends to ask about the probability of their *offspring* inheriting the mutation from the father. Given the options, the most plausible interpretation is that the question is asking about the inheritance pattern and the risk to offspring. Huntington disease is autosomal dominant, and each child of an affected parent has a 50% chance of inheriting the mutated allele.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect Inheritance Pattern & Probability.** Huntington disease is autosomal dominant, not recessive. Autosomal recessive requires two copies of the mutated gene. The probability for the wife inheriting the mutation is not 0% based on the provided information (she could have a *de novo* mutation, though rare, or the question might be poorly phrased). However, the inheritance pattern is definitely wrong.
- (B): **Incorrect Inheritance Pattern.** Huntington disease is autosomal, not X-linked. X-linked dominant inheritance would involve a gene on the X chromosome, and affected males would pass it to all daughters but no sons. The probability of 50% is correct for offspring risk, but the inheritance pattern is wrong.
- (D): **Incorrect Inheritance Pattern.** Mitochondrial inheritance involves genes in the mitochondrial DNA, passed down maternally. Huntington disease is caused by a nuclear gene (*HTT*) on chromosome 4. Mitochondrial disorders typically affect tissues with high energy demands (brain, muscle, heart). The probability of 100% is incorrect; the wife would only inherit the mutation if she already had it or developed a *de novo* mutation.
- (E): **Incorrect Inheritance Pattern & Probability.** Huntington disease is autosomal dominant, not recessive. The probability for the wife inheriting the mutation is not 50% based on the provided information (she could have a *de novo* mutation, though rare, or the question might be poorly phrased). However, the inheritance pattern is definitely wrong.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Huntington disease is a classic example of an autosomal dominant disorder caused by a trinucleotide repeat expansion. Remember the gene (*HTT*), chromosome location (4p), type of repeat (CAG), and the affected brain region (striatum/caudate nucleus). Key differentials for chorea include Sydenham's chorea (post-streptococcal), drug-induced chorea, Wilson's disease, and Huntington disease. Remember the inheritance pattern and the concept of anticipation (earlier onset and increased severity in successive generations due to further repeat expansion).

<a id='question-004'></a>

### Question 004: Marfan Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Marfan syndrome is an autosomal dominant connective tissue disorder caused by mutations in the *FBN1* gene, leading to defective fibrillin-1 protein, which affects the extracellular matrix, particularly in the aorta, ligaments, and lens of the eye. (Marfan 综合征是一种常染色体显性遗传的结缔组织疾病，由 *FBN1* 基因突变引起，导致纤维蛋白-1 缺陷，影响细胞外基质，特别是主动脉、韧带和眼睛晶状体。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of intermittent chest pain and shortness of breath with exertion. He reports a family history of "heart problems" in his mother, who died suddenly at age 45. On physical examination, he is tall and thin (6'5", 160 lbs) with long limbs and fingers (arachnodactyly). Cardiovascular examination reveals a harsh diastolic murmur heard best at the left sternal border. Ophthalmic examination shows upward and outward displacement of the lens (ectopia lentis). An echocardiogram reveals dilation of the aortic root (6.2 cm) and aortic regurgitation. Genetic testing confirms a heterozygous mutation in the *FBN1* gene on chromosome 15q21.1. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the aortic root dilation observed in this patient?

(A) Decreased synthesis of collagen type III, leading to weakened aortic wall structure.
(B) Impaired cross-linking of elastin fibers due to defective lysyl oxidase activity.
(C) Reduced formation of functional microfibrils, disrupting the structural integrity of the extracellular matrix.
(D) Increased activity of matrix metalloproteinases (MMPs) leading to excessive degradation of elastin.
(E) Defective synthesis of smooth muscle cells in the aortic media, resulting in decreased contractile force.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 28-year-old male with chest pain, shortness of breath, family history of early cardiac death, tall stature, arachnodactyly, diastolic murmur, ectopia lentis, dilated aortic root (6.2 cm), aortic regurgitation. These are classic features of Marfan syndrome.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Confirms heterozygous *FBN1* mutation. This directly links the phenotype to a defect in the fibrillin-1 protein.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining aortic root dilation. This requires understanding the role of fibrillin-1 in aortic wall structure and integrity.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Marfan Syndrome & FBN1:** Marfan syndrome is caused by mutations in the *FBN1* gene, which encodes fibrillin-1. Fibrillin-1 is a large glycoprotein (~350 kDa) that is a major component of extracellular microfibrils.
*   **Microfibrils:** These are structural components of the extracellular matrix (ECM), particularly abundant in connective tissues like the aorta, ligaments, and suspensory ligaments of the lens. Microfibrils serve as a scaffold for the deposition of other ECM components, including elastin and collagen. They also bind and sequester transforming growth factor-beta (TGF-β), regulating its bioavailability.
*   **Role in Aortic Wall:** In the aortic wall, microfibrils provide structural support and elasticity. They form a scaffold for elastin deposition, which is crucial for the aorta's ability to stretch and recoil during the cardiac cycle.
*   **FBN1 Mutation Effect:** Mutations in *FBN1* lead to the production of defective fibrillin-1 protein. This results in reduced amounts of functional fibrillin-1 and impaired formation or assembly of microfibrils.
*   **Consequence of Defective Microfibrils:** The lack of functional microfibrils disrupts the structural integrity of the ECM. This leads to weakened aortic walls, reduced elasticity, and increased susceptibility to dilation and aneurysm formation, particularly in the aortic root. The defective microfibrils also lead to increased TGF-β signaling, which can contribute to aortic pathology. The ectopia lentis is also due to defective fibrillin-1 in the suspensory ligaments of the lens. The skeletal features (tall stature, arachnodactyly) are related to fibrillin-1's role in skeletal development and connective tissue.
*   **Therefore:** The aortic root dilation in Marfan syndrome is primarily caused by the reduced formation of functional microfibrils due to defective fibrillin-1, which compromises the structural integrity of the aortic wall's ECM.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Decreased synthesis of collagen type III:** While collagen is a major component of the ECM, Marfan syndrome is specifically linked to *FBN1* (fibrillin-1), not collagen type III. Defects in collagen type III synthesis are associated with Ehlers-Danlos syndrome type IV (vascular type), which presents with arterial fragility and rupture, but the primary defect is not fibrillin-1. Fibrillin-1 mutations affect microfibril structure, which influences collagen and elastin deposition, but the primary defect isn't collagen III synthesis itself.
- (B): **Impaired cross-linking of elastin fibers due to defective lysyl oxidase activity:** Lysyl oxidase is essential for cross-linking collagen and elastin fibers, providing tensile strength and elasticity. Defective lysyl oxidase activity is associated with Menkes disease (copper deficiency) or Ehlers-Danlos syndrome type IX (ATP6AP1 mutations), leading to skin hyperextensibility and joint hypermobility, but not the specific constellation of features seen in Marfan syndrome (especially ectopia lentis and aortic root dilation due to microfibril defects).
- (D): **Increased activity of matrix metalloproteinases (MMPs) leading to excessive degradation of elastin:** While MMPs are involved in ECM remodeling and degradation, and their activity might be altered in Marfan syndrome, the primary defect is not increased MMP activity. The fundamental problem is the defective structural scaffold (microfibrils) provided by fibrillin-1, leading to weakened tissue. Increased MMP activity could be a secondary consequence or contribute to the pathology, but it's not the primary molecular mechanism stemming from the *FBN1* mutation.
- (E): **Defective synthesis of smooth muscle cells in the aortic media, resulting in decreased contractile force:** Smooth muscle cells are important for vascular tone and function, but the primary pathology in Marfan syndrome's aortic dilation involves the structural components of the ECM (microfibrils, elastin, collagen) in the media and adventitia, not a primary defect in smooth muscle cell synthesis or contractility. While smooth muscle dysfunction might occur secondarily, it's not the root cause linked to the *FBN1* mutation.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Marfan syndrome is caused by *FBN1* mutations leading to defective fibrillin-1 and impaired microfibril formation, weakening the ECM, especially in the aorta (causing dilation/aneurysm) and lens (causing ectopia lentis). Key differentials include Loeys-Dietz syndrome (TGFBR1/2 mutations, similar but often more severe aortic disease and craniofacial features) and Ehlers-Danlos syndromes (various types, affecting collagen synthesis or processing, leading to joint hypermobility, skin hyperextensibility, and vascular fragility, but typically without ectopia lentis or the specific microfibril defect of Marfan).

<a id='question-005'></a>

### Question 005: Neurofibromatosis Type 1 (von Recklinghausen Disease)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the molecular basis of Neurofibromatosis Type 1 (NF1), specifically the role of the NF1 gene product (neurofibromin) as a tumor suppressor protein and its function as a GTPase-activating protein (GAP) for Ras, leading to constitutive Ras activation and uncontrolled cell proliferation. (理解神经纤维瘤病1型(NF1)的分子基础，特别是NF1基因产物(神经纤维瘤蛋白)作为肿瘤抑制蛋白的作用，以及它作为Ras的GTP酶激活蛋白(GAP)的功能，导致Ras持续激活和不受控制的细胞增殖。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old female presents to her primary care physician complaining of multiple, painless, flesh-colored bumps appearing on her skin over the past few years. She also reports occasional headaches and mild lower back pain. Her mother has a similar condition, characterized by numerous skin lesions and optic gliomas diagnosed in childhood. Physical examination reveals multiple café-au-lait macules (>6, >1.5 cm in diameter) scattered across her trunk and limbs, several cutaneous neurofibromas, and axillary freckling (Crowe sign). Ophthalmoscopic examination reveals multiple Lisch nodules (iris hamartomas). A skin biopsy from a neurofibroma shows increased numbers of spindle cells with admixed lymphocytes and mast cells. Genetic testing confirms a heterozygous pathogenic variant in the NF1 gene located on chromosome 17q11.2. The patient is concerned about her risk of developing malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumors (MPNSTs). Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the pathogenesis of neurofibromas and the increased risk of MPNSTs in this patient?

(A) Loss of function of the APC tumor suppressor protein, leading to accumulation of β-catenin and increased transcription of Wnt target genes, resulting in uncontrolled cell proliferation in the colon epithelium.
(B) Loss of function of the PTEN tumor suppressor protein, leading to decreased production of PIP3 and increased activation of the PI3K/Akt/mTOR pathway, promoting cell growth and survival.
(C) Loss of function of the neurofibromin protein, leading to decreased GTPase activity towards Ras, resulting in constitutive activation of the Ras/MAPK signaling pathway and uncontrolled cell proliferation.
(D) Gain-of-function mutation in the RET proto-oncogene, leading to constitutive activation of the RET receptor tyrosine kinase and increased signaling through the MAPK pathway, causing medullary thyroid carcinoma and multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2.
(E) Loss of function of the VHL tumor suppressor protein, leading to stabilization of HIF-1α and increased transcription of genes involved in angiogenesis and erythropoiesis, resulting in hemangioblastomas and renal cell carcinoma.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 28-year-old female with multiple skin bumps (neurofibromas), café-au-lait spots, axillary freckling, Lisch nodules, headaches, back pain. These are classic signs of Neurofibromatosis Type 1 (NF1).
*   **Family History:** Mother has a similar condition, suggesting an autosomal dominant inheritance pattern.
*   **Physical Exam:** Confirms multiple café-au-lait spots (>6, >1.5 cm), cutaneous neurofibromas, Crowe sign, and Lisch nodules – diagnostic criteria for NF1.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Confirms a heterozygous pathogenic variant in the NF1 gene on chromosome 17q11.2. This confirms the diagnosis of NF1.
*   **Concern:** Increased risk of Malignant Peripheral Nerve Sheath Tumors (MPNSTs), a known complication of NF1.
*   **Question:** Asks for the molecular mechanism underlying the pathogenesis of neurofibromas and MPNST risk in NF1.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   NF1 is an autosomal dominant disorder caused by mutations in the NF1 gene, which encodes the protein neurofibromin.
*   Neurofibromin is a large protein with multiple domains, including a GTPase-activating protein (GAP) domain.
*   The GAP domain of neurofibromin stimulates the intrinsic GTPase activity of Ras proteins (H-Ras, N-Ras, K-Ras). Ras proteins are small GTPases that act as molecular switches in signal transduction pathways.
*   In their active, GTP-bound state, Ras proteins signal downstream through the MAPK (Mitogen-Activated Protein Kinase) pathway, promoting cell proliferation, differentiation, and survival.
*   Neurofibromin, by acting as a GAP, accelerates the hydrolysis of GTP bound to Ras, converting it to the inactive, GDP-bound state. This effectively acts as a brake on Ras signaling.
*   In NF1, loss-of-function mutations in the NF1 gene lead to reduced or absent neurofibromin protein.
*   Without functional neurofibromin GAP activity, Ras proteins remain in their active, GTP-bound state for longer periods, leading to constitutive (persistent) activation of the Ras/MAPK signaling pathway.
*   This uncontrolled activation of the MAPK pathway drives excessive cell proliferation and inhibits apoptosis, contributing to the formation of neurofibromas (benign tumors of Schwann cells and fibroblasts) and increasing the risk of malignant transformation into MPNSTs (sarcomas arising from peripheral nerve sheaths).
*   Therefore, the underlying molecular mechanism is the loss of neurofibromin's GAP activity towards Ras, resulting in constitutive Ras activation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Loss of APC function is characteristic of Familial Adenomatous Polyposis (FAP) and colorectal cancer. APC normally degrades β-catenin. Loss of APC leads to β-catenin accumulation, translocation to the nucleus, and activation of Wnt target genes promoting cell proliferation in the colon epithelium. This is unrelated to NF1 pathogenesis.
- (B): Loss of PTEN function is associated with Cowden syndrome and Prostate cancer, among others. PTEN is a phosphatase that antagonizes the PI3K pathway by dephosphorylating PIP2 to PIP3. Loss of PTEN leads to increased PIP3 levels, activating the PI3K/Akt/mTOR pathway, promoting cell growth and survival. This pathway is distinct from the Ras/MAPK pathway primarily affected in NF1.
- (D): Gain-of-function mutations in the RET proto-oncogene cause Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia type 2 (MEN2) syndromes (MEN2A, MEN2B, FMTC). RET is a receptor tyrosine kinase. Activating mutations lead to constitutive kinase activity and downstream signaling, primarily through the MAPK pathway, but in different tissues (thyroid, adrenal medulla, parathyroid) and due to a different primary genetic event (gain-of-function vs. loss-of-function tumor suppressor).
- (E): Loss of VHL function causes Von Hippel-Lindau disease. VHL is a tumor suppressor protein that targets HIF-1α for proteasomal degradation under normoxic conditions. Loss of VHL leads to HIF-1α stabilization, promoting angiogenesis and erythropoiesis, leading to characteristic tumors like hemangioblastomas (CNS, retina) and renal cell carcinoma. This mechanism is unrelated to NF1.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Neurofibromatosis Type 1 is caused by loss-of-function mutations in the NF1 gene, leading to deficient neurofibromin protein. Neurofibromin acts as a GAP for Ras GTPases, normally inactivating Ras. Loss of neurofibromin results in constitutive activation of the Ras/MAPK pathway, driving tumor formation (neurofibromas, MPNSTs). Key differentials include other neurocutaneous syndromes like Tuberous Sclerosis (TSC1/TSC2 loss, mTOR pathway) and Von Hippel-Lindau disease (VHL loss, HIF pathway). Remember NF1 involves Ras activation, while TSC involves mTOR activation, and VHL involves HIF activation.

<a id='question-006'></a>

### Question 006: Neurofibromatosis Type 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the genetic basis, clinical manifestations, and molecular mechanism of Neurofibromatosis Type 2 (NF2), specifically the role of the Merlin protein in tumor suppression and cell adhesion, and differentiating it from other neurocutaneous syndromes. (理解神经纤维瘤病2型 (NF2) 的遗传基础、临床表现和分子机制，特别是 Merlin 蛋白在肿瘤抑制和细胞粘附中的作用，并将其与其他神经皮肤综合征区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the neurology clinic complaining of progressive bilateral hearing loss and tinnitus over the past 5 years. She also reports occasional headaches. Her past medical history is unremarkable, but her father was diagnosed with acoustic neuromas in his 40s. On physical examination, pure-tone audiometry reveals sensorineural hearing loss bilaterally. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) of the brain shows bilateral vestibular schwannomas (acoustic neuromas) and a single meningioma. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous missense mutation in the *NF2* gene located on chromosome 22q12.2. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the pathogenesis of the vestibular schwannomas in this patient?

(A) Loss of function of the *RET* proto-oncogene, leading to constitutive activation of the RAS/MAPK pathway and uncontrolled cell proliferation.
(B) Haploinsufficiency of the *NF1* gene product, neurofibromin, resulting in impaired RAS regulation and increased cell growth.
(C) Loss of function of the Merlin protein, leading to disruption of cell adhesion, cytoskeletal organization, and tumor suppression pathways.
(D) Gain-of-function mutation in the *TP53* gene, resulting in impaired DNA repair and increased genomic instability, leading to tumor formation.
(E) Loss of function of the *PTEN* gene product, leading to decreased levels of phosphatidylinositol (3,4,5)-trisphosphate (PIP3) and reduced activation of the AKT/mTOR pathway.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old female with progressive bilateral hearing loss, tinnitus, and headaches. This constellation of symptoms, particularly bilateral hearing loss, strongly suggests bilateral vestibular schwannomas (acoustic neuromas).
- **Family History:** Father diagnosed with acoustic neuromas, indicating a possible inherited condition.
- **Imaging Findings:** MRI confirms bilateral vestibular schwannomas and a meningioma. This combination is highly characteristic of Neurofibromatosis Type 2 (NF2).
- **Genetic Testing:** Heterozygous missense mutation in the *NF2* gene on chromosome 22q12.2 confirms the diagnosis of NF2.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism underlying the pathogenesis of the vestibular schwannomas in this patient with NF2.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- NF2 is an autosomal dominant disorder caused by mutations in the *NF2* gene, which encodes the protein Merlin (also known as schwannomin).
- Merlin is a tumor suppressor protein that localizes to the cell membrane and cytoplasm.
- Its primary function is to act as a scaffold protein, interacting with various signaling molecules and cytoskeletal components.
- Merlin plays a crucial role in regulating cell adhesion, cell growth, and apoptosis. It interacts with proteins like ERB4 (a receptor tyrosine kinase) and the actin cytoskeleton, linking cell adhesion complexes (like adherens junctions) to intracellular signaling pathways.
- Loss of function of Merlin, typically through haploinsufficiency (as seen in most NF2 cases, including heterozygous mutations), leads to disruption of these normal cellular processes.
- Specifically, loss of Merlin function disrupts cell adhesion, alters cytoskeletal organization, and impairs tumor suppressor pathways, such as the Hippo pathway and the PI3K/AKT pathway.
- Schwann cells, which form the myelin sheath around peripheral nerves, are particularly sensitive to Merlin loss. The vestibular nerve is often affected first in NF2, leading to bilateral vestibular schwannomas. Meningiomas and ependymomas are also common tumors associated with NF2 due to Merlin's role in regulating cell growth in these tissues.
- Therefore, the pathogenesis of vestibular schwannomas in NF2 is primarily driven by the loss of function of the Merlin protein.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Loss of function of the *RET* proto-oncogene is associated with Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia type 2 (MEN2), characterized by medullary thyroid carcinoma, pheochromocytoma, and parathyroid hyperplasia. This is unrelated to NF2 and vestibular schwannomas. The RAS/MAPK pathway is involved in cell proliferation, but its constitutive activation in this context is due to *RET* gain-of-function mutations, not *NF2* loss-of-function.
- (B): Haploinsufficiency of the *NF1* gene product, neurofibromin, causes Neurofibromatosis Type 1 (NF1). Neurofibromin negatively regulates the RAS pathway by acting as a GTPase-activating protein (GAP) for RAS. Loss of neurofibromin leads to constitutive RAS activation and increased cell growth, resulting in neurofibromas, café-au-lait spots, Lisch nodules, and optic gliomas. This is distinct from NF2, which involves the *NF2* gene and Merlin protein.
- (D): Gain-of-function mutations in the *TP53* gene are associated with Li-Fraumeni syndrome, a hereditary cancer predisposition syndrome characterized by a wide range of tumors, including sarcomas, breast cancer, brain tumors, and adrenocortical carcinoma. *TP53* is a critical tumor suppressor involved in DNA repair, cell cycle arrest, and apoptosis. While *TP53* mutations can lead to tumor formation, they are not the primary cause of NF2. NF2 is caused by loss-of-function mutations in *NF2*, not gain-of-function mutations in *TP53*.
- (E): Loss of function of the *PTEN* gene product is associated with PTEN hamartoma tumor syndrome (PHTS), which includes Cowden syndrome and Bannayan-Riley-Ruvalcaba syndrome. PTEN is a phosphatase that negatively regulates the PI3K/AKT/mTOR pathway by dephosphorylating PIP3. Loss of PTEN leads to increased PIP3 levels and hyperactivation of the AKT/mTOR pathway, promoting cell growth and survival. This is unrelated to the molecular mechanism of NF2.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- NF2 is caused by mutations in the *NF2* gene, leading to loss of function of the Merlin tumor suppressor protein. This results in characteristic bilateral vestibular schwannomas, meningiomas, and ependymomas. (NF2 由 *NF2* 基因突变引起，导致 Merlin 肿瘤抑制蛋白功能丧失。这会导致特征性的双侧前庭神经鞘瘤、脑膜瘤和室管膜瘤。)
- Key differentials include NF1 (caused by *NF1* mutations, neurofibromin loss, different tumor types) and MEN2 (caused by *RET* mutations, different tumor types). (主要鉴别诊断包括 NF1（由 *NF1* 突变引起，神经纤维瘤蛋白丧失，不同类型的肿瘤）和 MEN2（由 *RET* 突变引起，不同类型的肿瘤）。)

<a id='question-007'></a>

### Question 007: Autosomal Dominant Polycystic Kidney Disease (ADPKD)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the clinical manifestations, genetic basis (PKD1 vs. PKD2), and associated complications of Autosomal Dominant Polycystic Kidney Disease (ADPKD), specifically distinguishing between the two major genes involved and their relative contributions to disease severity and specific complications. (理解多囊肾 (ADPKD) 的临床表现、遗传基础 (PKD1 vs PKD2)、相关并发症，特别是区分两个主要基因及其对疾病严重程度和特定并发症的相对贡献。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of severe flank pain radiating to the groin and hematuria. His medical history is significant for hypertension diagnosed 10 years ago, which is currently managed with lisinopril and amlodipine. He also reports recurrent urinary tract infections. His father died at age 65 from a ruptured intracranial aneurysm. On physical examination, the patient's blood pressure is 180/105 mmHg. Abdominal examination reveals palpable, enlarged kidneys bilaterally. Urinalysis shows microscopic hematuria and proteinuria (3+). Renal function tests reveal elevated serum creatinine (2.8 mg/dL) and BUN (45 mg/dL). An abdominal ultrasound confirms massively enlarged kidneys with multiple cysts throughout both renal parenchyma. A CT angiogram of the head is ordered due to the family history and reveals a 7 mm saccular outpouching at the bifurcation of the anterior communicating artery. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *PKD1* gene.

Which of the following is the MOST likely explanation for the patient's presentation and family history?

(A) The patient has Autosomal Recessive Polycystic Kidney Disease (ARPKD), characterized by mutations in the *PKHD1* gene, typically presenting in infancy with severe liver fibrosis and renal failure, and is not associated with intracranial aneurysms.
(B) The patient has ADPKD due to a mutation in the *PKD2* gene, which typically presents later in life with milder renal disease and a lower risk of intracranial aneurysms compared to *PKD1* mutations.
(C) The patient has ADPKD due to a mutation in the *PKD1* gene, which is associated with earlier onset, more severe renal disease, and a higher risk of intracranial aneurysms and hepatic cysts compared to *PKD2* mutations.
(D) The patient has nephronophthisis, an autosomal recessive disorder characterized by mutations in *NPHP* genes, leading to tubulointerstitial fibrosis and cysts, typically presenting in childhood or adolescence with proteinuria and progressive renal failure, but not associated with intracranial aneurysms or hepatic cysts.
(E) The patient has medullary cystic kidney disease (MCKD), an autosomal recessive disorder characterized by mutations in *MUC1* or *MUC4* genes, leading to cysts primarily in the medulla, typically presenting in adulthood with salt wasting, nephrogenic diabetes insipidus, and progressive renal failure, but not associated with intracranial aneurysms or hepatic cysts.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 52-year-old male with flank pain, hematuria, hypertension, recurrent UTIs, palpable enlarged kidneys, elevated creatinine/BUN, proteinuria, and multiple renal cysts on ultrasound. This constellation points towards Polycystic Kidney Disease (PKD).
*   **Family History:** Father died at 65 from a ruptured intracranial aneurysm. This is a known association with ADPKD.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Heterozygous mutation in the *PKD1* gene. This confirms the diagnosis of ADPKD and specifies the affected gene.
*   **Specific Findings:** Hypertension, renal cysts, intracranial aneurysm, and the patient's age of presentation (52 years old) are all characteristic features of ADPKD.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the explanation linking the patient's presentation, family history, and specific genetic finding (*PKD1* mutation).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **ADPKD:** Autosomal Dominant Polycystic Kidney Disease is the most common inherited kidney disorder. It is characterized by the progressive development and enlargement of numerous cysts in the kidneys, leading to renal failure. It is also associated with extrarenal manifestations, including hypertension, intracranial berry aneurysms, hepatic cysts, pancreatic cysts, and cardiac valve abnormalities.
*   **Genetics:** ADPKD is primarily caused by mutations in two genes: *PKD1* (located on chromosome 16p13.3) and *PKD2* (located on chromosome 4q21).
    *   *PKD1* mutations account for approximately 85% of ADPKD cases. They are generally associated with an earlier onset of disease (often in the 30s or 40s), more rapid progression to end-stage renal disease (ESRD), and a higher incidence of extrarenal manifestations, particularly intracranial aneurysms and hepatic cysts. The *PKD1* gene encodes polycystin-1, a large transmembrane protein involved in cell-cell and cell-matrix adhesion, mechanosensation, and calcium signaling.
    *   *PKD2* mutations account for approximately 15% of ADPKD cases. They are typically associated with a later onset of disease (often in the 50s or 60s), slower progression to ESRD, and a lower incidence of extrarenal manifestations compared to *PKD1* mutations. The *PKD2* gene encodes polycystin-2, an integral membrane protein that functions as a calcium channel.
*   **Patient's Case:** The patient presents with classic features of ADPKD at age 52, including hypertension, renal cysts, and renal insufficiency. The family history of an intracranial aneurysm is also consistent with ADPKD. Crucially, genetic testing confirms a mutation in the *PKD1* gene. This aligns with the typical presentation associated with *PKD1* mutations: earlier onset than typical *PKD2*, significant renal disease, and presence of an intracranial aneurysm.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Autosomal Recessive Polycystic Kidney Disease (ARPKD):** Incorrect. ARPKD is caused by mutations in *PKHD1* (not *PKD1* or *PKD2*), is autosomal recessive (not dominant), typically presents in infancy or childhood with severe liver fibrosis (congenital hepatic fibrosis) and renal failure, and is not associated with intracranial aneurysms. The patient's age of onset, autosomal dominant inheritance pattern (implied by father's history), lack of mention of liver fibrosis, and presence of an intracranial aneurysm rule out ARPKD.
*   **(B) ADPKD due to a mutation in the *PKD2* gene:** Incorrect. While *PKD2* mutations cause ADPKD, they are typically associated with a *later* onset (usually >50 years), *slower* disease progression, and a *lower* risk of intracranial aneurysms compared to *PKD1* mutations. The patient's presentation at age 52 with an intracranial aneurysm is more characteristic of a *PKD1* mutation.
*   **(D) Nephronophthisis:** Incorrect. Nephronophthisis is an autosomal recessive disorder (not dominant) caused by mutations in various *NPHP* genes. It primarily affects the tubules and interstitium, leading to tubulointerstitial fibrosis and cysts, typically presenting in childhood or adolescence. It is not associated with intracranial aneurysms or hepatic cysts. The patient's presentation (renal cysts, hypertension, adult onset, intracranial aneurysm) is inconsistent with nephronophthisis.
*   **(E) Medullary Cystic Kidney Disease (MCKD):** Incorrect. MCKD is an autosomal recessive disorder (not dominant) caused by mutations in *MUC1* or *MUC4* genes. It is characterized by cysts primarily located in the renal medulla, often presenting in adulthood with salt wasting, nephrogenic diabetes insipidus, and progressive renal failure. It is not associated with intracranial aneurysms or hepatic cysts. The patient's presentation (cortical cysts, hypertension, intracranial aneurysm) is inconsistent with MCKD.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
ADPKD is primarily caused by mutations in *PKD1* (85%) or *PKD2* (15%). *PKD1* mutations typically lead to earlier onset, more severe disease, and higher risk of intracranial aneurysms and hepatic cysts compared to *PKD2* mutations. Differentiate ADPKD from ARPKD (infancy onset, liver fibrosis, *PKHD1* gene), nephronophthisis (tubulointerstitial fibrosis, childhood onset, *NPHP* genes), and MCKD (medullary cysts, salt wasting, *MUC1/MUC4* genes). Remember the association between ADPKD and intracranial berry aneurysms.

<a id='question-008'></a>

### Question 008: Familial Hypercholesterolemia
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Autosomal dominant inheritance of mutations in the LDL receptor (LDLR) or Apolipoprotein B (APOB) gene leads to impaired clearance of LDL cholesterol from the circulation, resulting in severe hypercholesterolemia, premature atherosclerosis, and characteristic physical findings like tendon xanthomas. (常染色体显性遗传的LDLR或APOB基因突变导致LDL胆固醇清除受损，引起严重高胆固醇血症、过早动脉粥样硬化和肌腱黄瘤等特征性体征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of intermittent claudication in his left calf upon exertion. His father died at age 52 from a myocardial infarction, and his mother has a history of peripheral artery disease. On physical examination, the patient has visible yellowish deposits around his Achilles tendons bilaterally and on his extensor tendons of the hands. His blood pressure is 145/90 mmHg. Laboratory results show a total cholesterol level of 450 mg/dL, LDL cholesterol of 350 mg/dL, HDL cholesterol of 35 mg/dL, and triglycerides of 120 mg/dL. A genetic test reveals a heterozygous mutation in the LDLR gene located on chromosome 19.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms BEST explains the patient's elevated LDL cholesterol levels?

(A) Decreased synthesis of ApoB-100, leading to impaired assembly and secretion of VLDL particles.
(B) Impaired binding of LDL particles to the LDL receptor on hepatocytes due to a defect in the receptor's ligand-binding domain.
(C) Reduced activity of HMG-CoA reductase, resulting in decreased cholesterol synthesis and compensatory upregulation of LDL receptors.
(D) Defective synthesis of ApoE, leading to impaired clearance of chylomicron remnants and VLDL remnants (IDL).
(E) Increased expression of PCSK9, leading to accelerated degradation of LDL receptors.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 45-year-old male with claudication (suggesting peripheral artery disease), family history of premature cardiovascular disease (father's MI at 52, mother's PAD). This points towards a genetic predisposition to atherosclerosis.
- **Physical Exam:** Tendon xanthomas (yellowish deposits around tendons) are pathognomonic for severe hypercholesterolemia, specifically Familial Hypercholesterolemia (FH).
- **Lab Results:** Markedly elevated total cholesterol (450 mg/dL) and LDL cholesterol (350 mg/dL), normal triglycerides (120 mg/dL), and low HDL (35 mg/dL). This lipid profile is characteristic of FH, where LDL is disproportionately elevated compared to triglycerides.
- **Genetic Test:** Heterozygous mutation in the LDLR gene on chromosome 19 confirms the diagnosis of FH.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the elevated LDL cholesterol.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Familial Hypercholesterolemia (FH):** An autosomal dominant disorder caused primarily by mutations in the LDLR gene (most common), APOB gene (less common), or PCSK9 gene (rare).
- **LDLR Function:** The LDL receptor is a cell-surface receptor primarily expressed on hepatocytes. Its function is to bind LDL particles circulating in the bloodstream and internalize them via endocytosis. Inside the cell, LDL is broken down, releasing cholesterol, which is then used for cellular processes or stored.
- **Molecular Defect in FH (LDLR mutation):** The most common cause is a mutation in the LDLR gene. These mutations often affect the receptor's ability to bind LDL particles. The ligand-binding domain of the LDL receptor specifically recognizes ApoB-100, the apolipoprotein found on LDL particles. A defect in this domain prevents or reduces the binding of LDL to the receptor.
- **Consequence:** Reduced binding of LDL to the hepatocyte surface leads to decreased endocytosis and clearance of LDL from the circulation. This results in a buildup of LDL cholesterol in the plasma (hypercholesterolemia).
- **Pathophysiology:** The high circulating LDL levels lead to deposition of cholesterol in arterial walls, initiating and accelerating the process of atherosclerosis, leading to premature cardiovascular events (MI, stroke, PAD) as seen in the patient and his family. Tendon xanthomas are localized deposits of cholesterol in tendons.
- **Inheritance:** Autosomal dominant, meaning only one mutated copy of the gene is sufficient to cause the phenotype. The patient has a heterozygous mutation. Homozygous FH (two mutated copies) is much more severe.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Decreased synthesis of ApoB-100:** ApoB-100 is essential for the assembly and secretion of VLDL (Very Low-Density Lipoprotein) from the liver. Reduced ApoB-100 synthesis would impair VLDL secretion, leading to increased triglycerides and potentially decreased LDL (as VLDL is a precursor to LDL). This is incorrect; FH is characterized by high LDL and normal triglycerides. This mechanism is associated with conditions like abetalipoproteinemia (mutation in MTP) or hypobetalipoproteinemia (mutation in ApoB).
- (C) **Reduced activity of HMG-CoA reductase:** HMG-CoA reductase is the rate-limiting enzyme in cholesterol synthesis. Reduced activity (e.g., due to statin drugs or genetic defects) would decrease intracellular cholesterol synthesis. This would lead to compensatory upregulation of LDL receptors on the cell surface to increase cholesterol uptake from the circulation, resulting in *lower* LDL cholesterol levels. This is the opposite of what is seen in FH.
- (D) **Defective synthesis of ApoE:** ApoE is crucial for the clearance of chylomicron remnants (from dietary fat) and VLDL remnants (IDL - Intermediate-Density Lipoprotein) by the liver. Defective ApoE synthesis (e.g., in Type III hyperlipoproteinemia or dysbetalipoproteinemia) leads to accumulation of these remnant particles, resulting in elevated triglycerides and cholesterol, but not the specific LDL elevation and tendon xanthomas characteristic of FH.
- (E) **Increased expression of PCSK9:** PCSK9 (Proprotein Convertase Subtilisin/Kexin Type 9) is a protein that binds to LDL receptors on the hepatocyte surface and targets them for lysosomal degradation. Increased PCSK9 expression (either genetically or due to certain medications) leads to fewer LDL receptors on the cell surface, impairing LDL clearance and causing hypercholesterolemia. While this mechanism *does* cause hypercholesterolemia similar to FH, the vignette specifically states the patient has a mutation in the *LDLR* gene, not the PCSK9 gene. Therefore, while PCSK9 mutations can cause FH-like phenotypes, the *direct* mechanism in this patient, given the LDLR mutation, is related to the receptor itself, not its degradation pathway. Option (B) directly addresses the defect in the LDLR protein function.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Familial Hypercholesterolemia (FH) is a classic example of an autosomal dominant disorder caused by defects in the LDL clearance pathway. The primary defect is usually in the LDL receptor (LDLR) gene, leading to impaired binding of LDL particles to hepatocytes and subsequent hypercholesterolemia. Key features include very high LDL, normal triglycerides, premature atherosclerosis, and tendon xanthomas. Differentiating FH from other hyperlipidemias involves understanding the specific lipid profile and the underlying molecular defect (e.g., ApoB defects cause low LDL/VLDL, ApoE defects cause high remnants/triglycerides, HMG-CoA reductase defects cause low cholesterol).

<a id='question-009'></a>

### Question 009: Achondroplasia
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Achondroplasia is the most common form of skeletal dysplasia, caused by an autosomal dominant gain-of-function mutation in the *FGFR3* gene, leading to impaired endochondral ossification and characteristic rhizomelic dwarfism. (软骨发育不全是最常见的骨骼发育不良形式，由 *FGFR3* 基因的常染色体显性功能获得突变引起，导致内软骨成骨受损和典型的肢根型矮小。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old primigravida undergoes routine prenatal screening. Ultrasound at 20 weeks gestation reveals a fetus with shortened long bones, particularly the humerus and femur (rhizomelia), a relatively large head with frontal bossing, and a depressed nasal bridge. Amniocentesis is performed, and fetal karyotyping shows a normal 46,XY. Molecular genetic testing of fetal DNA reveals a heterozygous missense mutation in the *FGFR3* gene, specifically a glycine-to-arginine substitution at codon 380 (Gly380Arg). The parents are of average stature and have no family history of skeletal dysplasia. Postnatally, the infant is born with the characteristic features of achondroplasia, including disproportionately short limbs, macrocephaly, midface hypoplasia, and trident hands. Radiographs confirm rhizomelic shortening of the long bones and demonstrate narrowed interpedicular distance in the lumbar spine. The infant's growth rate is significantly slower than expected for age.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the pathogenesis of achondroplasia in this infant?

(A) The *FGFR3* Gly380Arg mutation leads to a loss-of-function of the receptor, impairing its ability to bind fibroblast growth factor (FGF) ligands, thereby inhibiting chondrocyte proliferation and differentiation in the growth plate.
(B) The *FGFR3* Gly380Arg mutation results in constitutive activation of the receptor tyrosine kinase activity, independent of FGF ligand binding, leading to excessive proliferation of chondrocyte progenitor cells and premature differentiation and fusion of growth plate chondrocytes.
(C) The *FGFR3* Gly380Arg mutation causes increased expression of the *FGFR3* gene, leading to an overabundance of the receptor protein on the surface of chondrocytes, which enhances signaling downstream of FGF binding.
(D) The *FGFR3* Gly380Arg mutation impairs the intracellular trafficking of the receptor, causing it to be retained in the endoplasmic reticulum and degraded, resulting in reduced signaling through the FGF pathway in the growth plate.
(E) The *FGFR3* Gly380Arg mutation leads to increased degradation of the receptor protein by ubiquitin-mediated proteasomal degradation, resulting in decreased signaling through the FGF pathway in the growth plate.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Prenatal Ultrasound:** Shortened long bones (rhizomelia), large head (macrocephaly), frontal bossing, depressed nasal bridge are classic features of achondroplasia.
- **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XY rules out chromosomal abnormalities like trisomy 21 (Down syndrome), which can also cause skeletal abnormalities.
- **Molecular Genetics:** Heterozygous missense mutation in *FGFR3* (Gly380Arg) is the specific genetic defect known to cause achondroplasia. The mutation is *de novo* (parents are unaffected).
- **Postnatal Findings:** Confirms classic features: short limbs, macrocephaly, midface hypoplasia, trident hands.
- **Radiographs:** Rhizomelic shortening and narrowed interpedicular distance are characteristic radiographic findings.
- **Growth Rate:** Slow growth is expected in achondroplasia due to impaired endochondral ossification.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the pathogenesis.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Achondroplasia is caused by a gain-of-function mutation in the *FGFR3* gene, which encodes fibroblast growth factor receptor 3. The most common mutation is a Gly380Arg substitution in the transmembrane domain. This mutation leads to constitutive activation of the receptor's tyrosine kinase activity, meaning the receptor is active even in the absence of its ligand, FGF. Normally, FGF binding to FGFR3 initiates a signaling cascade that promotes chondrocyte proliferation and differentiation in the growth plate. However, the constitutively active FGFR3 Gly380Arg mutation causes excessive and prolonged signaling. This leads to:
1.  **Excessive proliferation of chondrocyte progenitor cells:** This depletes the progenitor cell pool.
2.  **Premature differentiation and hypertrophy of chondrocytes:** This leads to an early transition from proliferating chondrocytes to hypertrophic chondrocytes.
3.  **Premature fusion of the growth plate:** The hypertrophic chondrocytes are normally replaced by bone matrix, but the premature transition and fusion disrupt the normal process of endochondral ossification, leading to impaired longitudinal bone growth, particularly in the long bones (rhizomelia).
The constitutive activation is due to the Gly380Arg mutation disrupting the negative regulation of the receptor's kinase domain. Glycine at position 380 is thought to be involved in maintaining the inactive conformation of the kinase domain; replacing it with arginine, a positively charged amino acid, stabilizes the active conformation. This mechanism is a classic example of a dominant negative or gain-of-function mutation causing a skeletal dysplasia.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): This describes a *loss-of-function* mutation. Loss-of-function mutations in *FGFR3* cause different skeletal dysplasias, such as thanatophoric dysplasia (lethal) or hypochondroplasia (milder form), but not achondroplasia. Achondroplasia is caused by *gain-of-function*. The mutation *increases* receptor activity, not impairs ligand binding or inhibits proliferation/differentiation.
- (C): While the *FGFR3* gene expression might be affected in some conditions, the primary mechanism in achondroplasia is the constitutive activation of the *existing* receptor protein due to the specific Gly380Arg mutation, not simply increased expression levels. Increased expression alone wouldn't necessarily lead to constitutive activation.
- (D): This describes a mechanism where the receptor is retained in the ER and degraded, leading to reduced signaling. This is a *loss-of-function* mechanism, inconsistent with the gain-of-function nature of the achondroplasia mutation. The Gly380Arg mutation leads to a *functional* receptor that is constitutively active, not one that is misfolded and degraded.
- (E): This describes increased degradation of the receptor protein, also a *loss-of-function* mechanism. The Gly380Arg mutation results in a stable, constitutively active receptor, not one that is rapidly degraded.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Achondroplasia is caused by a gain-of-function mutation in *FGFR3*. This leads to constitutive activation of the receptor tyrosine kinase, inhibiting chondrocyte proliferation and promoting premature differentiation/fusion in the growth plate, resulting in rhizomelic dwarfism. Key differentials include other skeletal dysplasias like hypochondroplasia (less severe, different *FGFR3* mutations), thanatophoric dysplasia (lethal, different *FGFR3* mutations), and diastrophic dysplasia (different gene, *SLC26A2*). Remember that achondroplasia is typically autosomal dominant, with ~80% of cases arising from *de novo* mutations.

<a id='question-010'></a>

### Question 010: Hereditary Spherocytosis
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular basis, clinical manifestations, and laboratory findings of Hereditary Spherocytosis (HS), specifically focusing on the role of red blood cell membrane proteins (ankyrin, band 3, spectrin) in maintaining RBC shape and integrity, and the consequences of their deficiency. (考察红细胞膜蛋白缺陷导致红细胞形态异常、溶血的机制及临床表现)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old woman presents to the emergency department complaining of fatigue, jaundice, and dark urine for the past 2 days. She reports a history of similar episodes, often triggered by viral infections. Her past medical history is significant for splenectomy performed 5 years ago due to symptomatic splenomegaly and recurrent anemia. Family history reveals that her mother and younger brother also have similar symptoms and required splenectomy. Physical examination shows scleral icterus and mild hepatomegaly. Laboratory results show: Hemoglobin 9.5 g/dL (normal 12-16 g/dL), MCV 80 fL (normal 80-100 fL), RDW 18% (normal 11.5-14.5%), Reticulocyte count 8% (normal 0.5-2.5%), Total bilirubin 3.0 mg/dL (normal <1.2 mg/dL), Indirect bilirubin 2.5 mg/dL (normal <0.8 mg/dL), LDH 550 U/L (normal <250 U/L), Haptoglobin <10 mg/dL (normal 30-200 mg/dL), and a peripheral blood smear showing numerous spherocytes. An osmotic fragility test reveals increased fragility of her red blood cells in hypotonic saline solutions compared to normal controls.

Which of the following molecular defects is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Deficiency of Glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) leading to oxidative stress-induced hemolysis.
(B) Defect in the synthesis of alpha-globin chains, resulting in unstable hemoglobin and ineffective erythropoiesis.
(C) Mutation in the gene encoding ankyrin, disrupting the vertical interaction between the red blood cell membrane skeleton and the lipid bilayer.
(D) Autoantibodies directed against red blood cell surface antigens, causing immune-mediated extravascular hemolysis.
(E) Deficiency of pyruvate kinase, impairing ATP production and leading to decreased red blood cell deformability.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 28-year-old woman with fatigue, jaundice, dark urine (suggesting hemolysis).
*   **History:** Recurrent episodes triggered by infections, splenectomy 5 years ago for splenomegaly and anemia, family history of similar condition requiring splenectomy (suggests inherited disorder).
*   **Physical Exam:** Scleral icterus (jaundice), mild hepatomegaly (can be seen in chronic hemolysis).
*   **Lab Findings:**
    *   Anemia (Hb 9.5 g/dL).
    *   Normal MCV (80 fL) - rules out macrocytic anemias.
    *   High RDW (18%) - indicates anisocytosis (variation in RBC size), common in hemolytic anemias.
    *   High Reticulocyte count (8%) - indicates bone marrow compensation for hemolysis.
    *   Elevated Indirect Bilirubin (2.5 mg/dL) - product of heme breakdown from hemolysis.
    *   Elevated LDH (550 U/L) - released from lysed RBCs.
    *   Low Haptoglobin (<10 mg/dL) - binds free hemoglobin released during hemolysis.
    *   Peripheral smear: Spherocytes (small, dense, spherical RBCs lacking central pallor).
    *   Osmotic Fragility Test: Increased fragility (RBCs lyse more easily in hypotonic solutions).
*   **Splenectomy:** Performed due to symptomatic splenomegaly and anemia, a common treatment for HS as the spleen removes abnormal spherocytes.
*   **Family History:** Suggests an autosomal dominant inheritance pattern, typical for HS.

These findings collectively point towards a hemolytic anemia characterized by spherocytes, increased osmotic fragility, and a family history, strongly suggesting Hereditary Spherocytosis.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Hereditary Spherocytosis (HS) is the most common inherited red blood cell membrane disorder. It is typically inherited in an autosomal dominant pattern (about 75% of cases), although autosomal recessive forms exist. The underlying defect involves mutations in genes encoding proteins that form the vertical linkages between the red blood cell membrane skeleton (primarily spectrin, ankyrin, protein 4.2, and actin) and the lipid bilayer (primarily band 3 and glycophorin C).

The most common mutations (affecting ~50-60% of cases) occur in the *ANK1* gene, which encodes ankyrin. Ankyrin is a crucial protein that links the spectrin-actin skeleton to band 3 (an integral membrane protein) and protein 4.2. Mutations in *ANK1* disrupt this vertical interaction, leading to destabilization of the membrane skeleton. Other common mutations occur in the *SLC4A1* gene (encoding band 3, ~20-25% of cases), *SPTA1* (encoding alpha-spectrin, ~5-10% of cases), *SPTB* (encoding beta-spectrin, ~5% of cases), and *EPB42* (encoding protein 4.2, ~5% of cases).

The loss of these membrane proteins leads to a reduction in the surface area of the red blood cell membrane relative to its volume. This causes the cell to lose its normal biconcave disc shape and adopt a spherical shape (spherocyte). Spherocytes are less deformable than normal RBCs and are prematurely removed from circulation by macrophages in the spleen (extravascular hemolysis). The spleen also enlarges (splenomegaly) due to the increased workload of removing these abnormal cells. The increased breakdown of hemoglobin leads to elevated indirect bilirubin, LDH, and decreased haptoglobin. The bone marrow compensates by increasing red blood cell production (reticulocytosis). The osmotic fragility test is positive because spherocytes, having less surface area, are more susceptible to lysis when placed in hypotonic solutions.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Deficiency of Glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD)** leads to hemolytic anemia, often triggered by oxidative stress (e.g., infections, drugs). While it causes hemolysis, the characteristic RBC morphology is typically bite cells or Heinz bodies (visible with special stains), not primarily spherocytes. The osmotic fragility test is usually normal. G6PD deficiency is X-linked recessive, not typically autosomal dominant with splenectomy.
- (B): **Defect in the synthesis of alpha-globin chains** (e.g., alpha-thalassemia) leads to an excess of beta-globin chains, which can precipitate and damage RBC precursors (ineffective erythropoiesis) and mature RBCs. This results in microcytic, hypochromic anemia, not typically spherocytes or increased osmotic fragility. Hemolysis, if present, is usually less severe than in HS.
- (D): **Autoantibodies directed against red blood cell surface antigens** cause Autoimmune Hemolytic Anemia (AIHA). This leads to extravascular hemolysis (primarily in the spleen) or intravascular hemolysis (depending on antibody type and complement fixation). The characteristic finding is a positive direct antiglobulin test (DAT or Coombs test), which is absent in HS. RBC morphology can vary but spherocytes can be seen, but the underlying mechanism is immune-mediated, not a genetic membrane protein defect.
- (E): **Deficiency of pyruvate kinase (PK)** is the most common enzyme deficiency causing hereditary nonspherocytic hemolytic anemia. PK deficiency impairs ATP production in RBCs, leading to decreased ion pump activity, cell dehydration, and reduced deformability. While it causes chronic hemolytic anemia with jaundice and splenomegaly, the characteristic RBC morphology is echinocytes (burr cells) or stomatocytes, not spherocytes. The osmotic fragility test is typically normal or decreased.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hereditary Spherocytosis is characterized by defects in RBC membrane proteins (ankyrin, band 3, spectrin, protein 4.2), leading to spherocyte formation, extravascular hemolysis, splenomegaly, and increased osmotic fragility. It is typically autosomal dominant. Key differentials include other causes of hemolytic anemia like G6PD deficiency (oxidative stress trigger, bite cells), thalassemia (microcytic anemia), autoimmune hemolytic anemia (positive DAT), and pyruvate kinase deficiency (nonspherocytic hemolysis, echinocytes).
