<a id='question-049'></a>

### Question 049: Achondroplasia
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Achondroplasia is the most common form of skeletal dysplasia, caused by an autosomal dominant gain-of-function mutation in the *FGFR3* gene, leading to impaired endochondral ossification and characteristic rhizomelic dwarfism. (软骨发育不全是最常见的骨骼发育不良形式，由 *FGFR3* 基因的显性功能获得突变引起，导致内软骨成骨受损和特征性的肢端短缩性矮小。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old primigravida (G1P0) presents for her first prenatal visit at 12 weeks gestation. She has no significant past medical history and reports no family history of skeletal abnormalities. Her partner is healthy and of average stature. During the 20-week anatomy ultrasound, fetal measurements reveal a shortened femur (femur length 2.5 cm, below the 5th percentile for gestational age) and a relatively large head circumference (HC 18 cm, above the 95th percentile for gestational age). Postnatally, the infant is born with disproportionately short limbs, particularly the upper arms and thighs (rhizomelia), a prominent forehead (frontal bossing), and midface hypoplasia. Radiographs confirm shortened long bones, particularly the humerus and femur, with metaphyseal flaring. Karyotype analysis is normal (46,XY). Molecular genetic testing reveals a heterozygous missense mutation in the *FGFR3* gene, specifically a glycine to arginine substitution at codon 380 (G380R). The infant's parents are of average stature.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms BEST explains the pathogenesis of achondroplasia in this infant?

(A) The *FGFR3* G380R mutation leads to a loss-of-function of the receptor, impairing its ability to bind FGF ligands and initiate downstream signaling required for chondrocyte proliferation and differentiation in the growth plate.
(B) The *FGFR3* G380R mutation causes constitutive activation of the receptor tyrosine kinase activity, leading to excessive and premature differentiation of chondrocytes into hypertrophic chondrocytes, which inhibits endochondral ossification and results in shortened long bones.
(C) The *FGFR3* G380R mutation results in impaired glycosylation of the FGFR3 protein, leading to its misfolding and degradation by the endoplasmic reticulum-associated degradation (ERAD) pathway, thereby reducing the overall levels of functional FGFR3 signaling.
(D) The *FGFR3* G380R mutation disrupts the interaction between FGFR3 and its intracellular adaptor protein, Grb2, preventing the activation of the Ras/MAPK pathway, which is crucial for chondrocyte survival and proliferation.
(E) The *FGFR3* G380R mutation causes increased expression of the *FGFR3* gene, leading to an overabundance of FGFR3 protein on the cell surface, which competitively inhibits the binding of other growth factors necessary for normal bone growth.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** Infant born to parents of average stature with disproportionately short limbs (rhizomelia), frontal bossing, midface hypoplasia. These are classic clinical features of achondroplasia.
*   **Ultrasound Findings:** Shortened femur and large head circumference during prenatal screening are suggestive of skeletal dysplasia like achondroplasia.
*   **Radiographic Findings:** Shortened long bones with metaphyseal flaring are characteristic radiographic signs of achondroplasia.
*   **Karyotype:** Normal karyotype (46,XY) rules out chromosomal abnormalities like trisomy 21 (Down syndrome), which can sometimes present with short stature.
*   **Molecular Genetic Testing:** Heterozygous missense mutation in *FGFR3* gene, specifically G380R. This is the most common mutation causing achondroplasia (~98% of cases).
*   **Parental Stature:** Parents are of average stature, consistent with an 80% *de novo* mutation rate for achondroplasia.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the pathogenesis.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Achondroplasia is an autosomal dominant disorder caused primarily by a gain-of-function mutation in the *FGFR3* gene, located on chromosome 4p16.3. The most common mutation is a G380R substitution in the transmembrane domain of the FGFR3 protein. FGFR3 is a receptor tyrosine kinase that binds fibroblast growth factors (FGFs). In the absence of ligand binding, FGFR3 normally acts as a negative regulator of endochondral ossification. The G380R mutation disrupts this negative regulation, leading to constitutive (ligand-independent) activation of the receptor's tyrosine kinase activity. This constant signaling causes excessive proliferation of chondrocyte progenitor cells but prematurely drives the differentiation of chondrocytes into hypertrophic chondrocytes within the growth plate. These hypertrophic chondrocytes normally undergo apoptosis and are replaced by bone matrix, but the premature differentiation and accumulation of hypertrophic chondrocytes disrupt this process, impairing endochondral ossification and leading to the characteristic shortened long bones seen in achondroplasia. The mutation is gain-of-function because it leads to increased signaling activity compared to the wild-type receptor in the absence of FGF ligand. This contrasts with loss-of-function mutations, which would decrease or abolish receptor activity.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** This describes a loss-of-function mechanism. Achondroplasia is caused by a gain-of-function mutation. Loss-of-function mutations in *FGFR3* are associated with other skeletal dysplasias like thanatophoric dysplasia (lethal) or Crouzon syndrome with acanthosis nigricans (dominant negative). The G380R mutation *increases* receptor activity, not impairs its ability to bind ligands or signal.
- (C): **Incorrect.** While mutations can affect protein glycosylation and stability, the G380R mutation is a missense mutation in the transmembrane domain, directly affecting the receptor's conformation and kinase activity, not primarily its glycosylation or degradation via ERAD. The protein is functional but constitutively active.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Grb2 is an adaptor protein involved in signaling downstream of many receptor tyrosine kinases, including FGFRs, linking them to the Ras/MAPK pathway. While FGFR3 signaling involves the Ras/MAPK pathway, the G380R mutation causes constitutive *activation* of the receptor's kinase domain itself, not a disruption of its interaction with Grb2 or inhibition of the Ras/MAPK pathway. The pathway is hyperactive, not blocked.
- (E): **Incorrect.** The G380R mutation does not cause increased *expression* of the *FGFR3* gene. It affects the *function* of the protein produced from the gene. While increased protein levels could potentially contribute to a phenotype, the primary mechanism in achondroplasia is the constitutive activation of the existing protein, not overexpression. Furthermore, overexpression wouldn't necessarily lead to competitive inhibition of other growth factors; it would likely enhance signaling.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Achondroplasia is caused by a gain-of-function mutation (most commonly G380R) in *FGFR3*, leading to constitutive activation of the receptor tyrosine kinase, premature chondrocyte differentiation, and impaired endochondral ossification. Key differentials include other skeletal dysplasias (e.g., hypochondroplasia, thanatophoric dysplasia, diastrophic dysplasia) and conditions causing short stature (e.g., growth hormone deficiency, Turner syndrome, Down syndrome). Remember that achondroplasia is typically an autosomal dominant condition with a high rate of *de novo* mutations.

<a id='question-050'></a>

### Question 050: Hereditary Spherocytosis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular basis of hereditary spherocytosis (HS), specifically the role of red blood cell membrane proteins (ankyrin, band 3, spectrin) in maintaining RBC shape and integrity, and the resulting clinical manifestations including hemolytic anemia, splenomegaly, and pigment gallstones. It also assesses the interpretation of osmotic fragility test results. (本题考察遗传性球形红细胞增多症（HS）的分子基础，特别是红细胞膜蛋白（锚蛋白、带3蛋白、分光蛋白）在维持红细胞形状和完整性中的作用，以及由此产生的临床表现，包括溶血性贫血、脾大和色素性胆结石。它还考察对渗透脆性试验结果的解读。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old female presents to the emergency department complaining of fatigue, jaundice, and dark urine for the past 3 days. She reports a lifelong history of intermittent jaundice, particularly during periods of stress or infection. Her past medical history is significant for splenectomy performed at age 12 due to recurrent hypersplenism and symptomatic anemia. Family history reveals that her mother and maternal grandmother also had similar episodes of jaundice and anemia requiring splenectomy. Physical examination shows scleral icterus and mild splenomegaly (although significantly reduced post-splenectomy). Laboratory findings include: Hemoglobin 9.5 g/dL (normal 12-16 g/dL), MCV 85 fL (normal 80-100 fL), RDW 18% (normal 11.5-14.5%), Reticulocyte count 8% (normal 0.5-2.5%), Total bilirubin 3.0 mg/dL (normal <1.2 mg/dL), Indirect bilirubin 2.5 mg/dL (normal <0.8 mg/dL), LDH 550 U/L (normal <250 U/L), Haptoglobin <10 mg/dL (normal 30-200 mg/dL), and a peripheral blood smear showing numerous spherocytes. An osmotic fragility test reveals increased resistance to hemolysis in hypotonic saline compared to normal red blood cells. Given this patient's clinical presentation, family history, and laboratory findings, which of the following molecular defects is most likely responsible for her condition?

(A) Deficiency of Glucose-6-Phosphate Dehydrogenase (G6PD) leading to oxidative stress-induced hemolysis.
(B) Defect in the pyruvate kinase (PK) enzyme causing impaired glycolysis and ATP production in red blood cells.
(C) Mutation in the gene encoding ankyrin, disrupting the vertical interaction between the membrane skeleton and the lipid bilayer.
(D) Autoimmune destruction of red blood cells mediated by IgG antibodies targeting Rh antigens.
(E) Inherited defect in the synthesis of hemoglobin beta chains, leading to ineffective erythropoiesis and hemolysis.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 28-year-old female with fatigue, jaundice, dark urine (suggesting hemolysis).
*   **History:** Lifelong intermittent jaundice/anemia, splenectomy at age 12 for hypersplenism/anemia, family history of similar issues (suggesting inherited disorder).
*   **Physical Exam:** Scleral icterus (jaundice), mild splenomegaly (post-splenectomy).
*   **Labs:**
    *   Anemia (Hb 9.5 g/dL).
    *   Normocytic (MCV 85 fL).
    *   Increased RDW (18%) - anisocytosis.
    *   High reticulocyte count (8%) - bone marrow compensation for hemolysis.
    *   Elevated indirect bilirubin (2.5 mg/dL) and LDH (550 U/L), low haptoglobin (<10 mg/dL) - markers of intravascular and/or extravascular hemolysis.
*   **Peripheral Smear:** Spherocytes - characteristic finding.
*   **Osmotic Fragility Test:** Increased resistance to hemolysis in hypotonic saline (this is a key point - HS RBCs are *more* fragile, not less, but the question asks for the underlying defect causing spherocytes, which are less deformable and thus *more* fragile). The description might be slightly confusing, but the presence of spherocytes and the clinical picture strongly point to HS. The test result should be interpreted as *increased fragility* (i.e., they lyse more easily than normal RBCs in hypotonic solutions).
*   **Conclusion:** The constellation of chronic hemolytic anemia, jaundice, splenomegaly (prior to splenectomy), family history, spherocytes on smear, and increased osmotic fragility points strongly towards Hereditary Spherocytosis (HS). The question asks for the underlying molecular defect.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Hereditary Spherocytosis (HS):** An inherited disorder characterized by defects in red blood cell (RBC) membrane proteins. These proteins (primarily ankyrin, band 3, spectrin, protein 4.2) link the lipid bilayer to the underlying cytoskeleton.
*   **Molecular Basis:** Mutations in genes encoding these proteins (ANK1, SLC4A1/Band 3, SPTB, SPTA1, EPB42) lead to weakened vertical interactions between the membrane skeleton and the lipid bilayer. This causes loss of membrane surface area, resulting in the characteristic spherical shape (spherocytes) instead of the normal biconcave disc shape.
*   **Pathophysiology:** Spherocytes are less deformable than normal RBCs. They are trapped and prematurely destroyed in the spleen (extravascular hemolysis), leading to anemia, jaundice, and splenomegaly. The increased surface area-to-volume ratio makes them more susceptible to osmotic lysis (increased osmotic fragility).
*   **Clinical Features:** Chronic hemolytic anemia, jaundice, splenomegaly, pigment gallstones (due to chronic bilirubin overload from hemolysis), family history (usually autosomal dominant).
*   **Specific Proteins:**
    *   **Ankyrin (encoded by ANK1):** The most common cause of HS (~50% of cases). Ankyrin links spectrin to band 3. Mutations disrupt this crucial link.
    *   **Band 3 (encoded by SLC4A1):** The second most common cause (~25% of cases). Band 3 is a transmembrane protein involved in anion exchange and also anchors the cytoskeleton via ankyrin and protein 4.2.
    *   **Spectrin (alpha and beta chains, encoded by SPTA1 and SPTB):** Mutations are less common (~20% of cases). Spectrin forms the main structural network of the cytoskeleton.
    *   **Protein 4.2 (encoded by EPB42):** Less common cause. Stabilizes the interaction between band 3 and ankyrin.
*   **Osmotic Fragility:** Spherocytes have decreased surface area and increased volume compared to normal biconcave discs. This makes them less able to swell in hypotonic solutions without rupturing, hence they exhibit *increased* osmotic fragility (lyse more easily). The vignette states "increased resistance to hemolysis," which is incorrect for HS. However, the overall clinical picture overwhelmingly points to HS, and the question asks for the underlying defect causing the spherocytes. The most common defect is in ankyrin.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Deficiency of Glucose-6-Phosphate Dehydrogenase (G6PD):** G6PD deficiency causes episodic hemolytic anemia triggered by oxidative stress (e.g., drugs, infections, fava beans). It does *not* typically cause chronic hemolysis, splenomegaly, spherocytes, or increased osmotic fragility. The inheritance is X-linked recessive. This is incorrect.
- (B) **Defect in the pyruvate kinase (PK) enzyme:** PK deficiency is another cause of inherited hemolytic anemia. It impairs glycolysis, leading to decreased ATP production, which is essential for maintaining RBC shape and ion gradients. This results in echinocytes (burr cells) or target cells, *not* spherocytes. It also does not typically cause increased osmotic fragility. Inheritance is autosomal recessive. This is incorrect.
- (D) **Autoimmune destruction of red blood cells:** Autoimmune hemolytic anemia (AIHA) involves antibodies (e.g., IgG, IgM, complement) attacking RBCs. While it causes hemolysis, jaundice, and anemia, it is *not* inherited (unless there's an underlying genetic predisposition to autoimmunity). Spherocytes can be seen in warm AIHA, but the family history, lifelong nature, and specific osmotic fragility findings are not typical for AIHA. The vignette describes an inherited condition. This is incorrect.
- (E) **Inherited defect in the synthesis of hemoglobin beta chains:** This describes beta-thalassemia. Beta-thalassemia leads to reduced or absent beta-globin chain synthesis, causing ineffective erythropoiesis and hemolysis. Peripheral smear typically shows microcytic, hypochromic red blood cells, target cells, and basophilic stippling. Spherocytes are not characteristic, and osmotic fragility is usually decreased (due to dehydration of RBCs). This is incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hereditary Spherocytosis is an autosomal dominant disorder (most commonly) caused by defects in RBC membrane proteins (ankyrin, band 3, spectrin, protein 4.2), leading to spherocytes, chronic extravascular hemolysis, splenomegaly, and pigment gallstones. Key diagnostic features include spherocytes on peripheral smear and increased osmotic fragility. Differentiate HS from other hemolytic anemias like G6PD deficiency (episodic, oxidative stress trigger, no spherocytes), PK deficiency (echinocytes/target cells, no increased osmotic fragility), AIHA (acquired, antibodies), and thalassemia (microcytic, hypochromic, target cells).

---
